At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nI guess intimacy can be a complicated subject in general, and it’s a little uncomfortable to talk about, but in terms of the eating itself… no, I don’t have a specific link I can point to. If there is one, I haven’t recognized it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear decrease, like I could say, “Yes, it used to be one way and now it’s different.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head.\n\nI guess I can say it hasn’t really stood out to me as a specific problem recently. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. It’s hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any connection between your eating episodes and your feelings about sex or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as being connected in a direct way.\n\nI mean, I can see why you’re asking, because they’re both kind of personal and they both have to do with how I feel in my body, or how comfortable I feel, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed, like, if something happens around sex or intimacy, then I have an eating episode afterward. I can’t point to that kind of pattern.\n\nThere are times where I feel more distant or not very connected, and there are times where I eat in a way that feels out of control, but I don’t know that one causes the other. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. It’s not something I’m aware of as a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel emotionally upset right before one of these eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m usually emotionally upset right before. Sometimes I might be, but it’s not like I can consistently look back and say, “Yes, I was really upset, and then that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more unclear than that. Like I might feel kind of off, or bored, or restless, or just not very settled in myself, but I don’t always recognize it as being emotionally upset in the moment. And sometimes it starts almost without me thinking very much at all, and then afterward I’m the one trying to analyze it, like, “Was I upset? Was something bothering me? Did something happen earlier?” But I’m not always sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes there’s something emotional there, but I haven’t noticed that I’m usually upset right before. It’s more like I realize something wasn’t quite right after the fact, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can name one specific emotional state, no. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it’s always when I’m sad,” or “it’s always when I’m anxious,” or something like that. But it doesn’t seem that clear to me.\n\nSometimes before it happens I might feel kind of unsettled, or like I’m avoiding something, or just not comfortable in my own head, but other times I don’t notice anything in particular until afterward. Then I start replaying the day and wondering, “Was I upset? Was I lonely? Was I frustrated?” But in the moment, it’s not always obvious.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific feeling that reliably comes before it. It feels more mixed up than that, or maybe I’m just not good at recognizing it before it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. It’s not something I’m aware of as a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel emotionally upset right before one of these eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m usually emotionally upset right before. I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t know if I’d call it a lack of interest exactly, or if it’s more that it just isn’t something I think about very much, or it depends on what else is going on with me.

Most of the time… I guess I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I also haven’t really tracked it that way, like “how often am I interested versus not interested.” It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured in my head. Sometimes I think if someone asked me out of nowhere, I’d say I’m not that interested, but then other times it might not feel so absolute.

I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer. I think I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, and I’m not sure whether it’s most of the time or just certain times. It’s kind of hard for me to separate interest in sex from how I’m feeling generally, or how comfortable I feel, or just whether I even have the mental space for it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m really scared by what happens when I start eating sometimes. It’s not just, you know, “I had a snack” or “I ate more than I meant to.” It’s like once I start, especially if I’m by myself in the kitchen, something just shifts. I feel like I’m watching myself do it, almost like I’ve stepped aside, and I can’t get myself to stop.\n\nAnd the whole time, I’m thinking, “Janet, put it away. Close the cabinet. Walk out of the kitchen.” I’m saying that in my head over and over, and I still keep going. That’s the part that really scares me, because it feels like I’m not in charge of myself in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like, and that’s the part that scares me the most. It’s not like I’m just choosing to have a little more and then I’m done. Once I start, especially if I’m alone in the kitchen, it feels like something kind of takes over, or like I’ve stepped aside from myself.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the cabinet open or at the counter, and in my head I’m saying, Janet, put it away. Stop. Close it. Walk out. And I really mean it — I’m not pretending I want to stop. I do want to stop. But my hands keep reaching, I keep eating, and it feels like I can’t make myself listen to me. It’s almost like there’s this gap between what I’m telling myself to do and what I’m actually doing, and I don’t know how to close it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… awful. Really awful. It’s not even just that my stomach feels bad or that I ate too much — it’s more this heavy feeling about myself, like, “What is wrong with me?” I’ll look at what’s gone, and sometimes it’s food I bought for the house or something I meant to share, and then I feel terrible, like I’ve wasted it or taken it from someone else even if no one says that.\n\nAnd then I get this shame, just sitting there with me. I’ll think, “Janet, you are too old to be acting like this. You’re a grown woman. Why can’t you just stop?” It’s embarrassing even saying that out loud, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nUsually I just sit there afterward and feel ashamed, and I tell myself this is the last time. I make these promises to myself, like, “Tomorrow I’m going to be normal, I’m not going to do this again, I’m going to handle it better.” But in that moment after, mostly I just feel disgusted with myself and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during or after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Mostly after, though honestly sometimes even while it’s happening there’s this voice in my head already telling me, “You’re going to feel terrible about this,” but I still keep going. Afterward is when it really lands.\n\nI’ll look at what I ate and think about how some of it was food I meant to have for later, or something I bought for the house, or even something I thought I might share, and then I feel just awful. Like I’ve wasted it, or like I’ve done something selfish, even if no one else is there saying that to me. I say it to myself.\n\nAnd then there’s this shame that comes over me. I’ll sit there thinking, “Janet, you’re 48 years old. You are too old to be acting this way. Why can’t you just stop?” It makes me feel childish and embarrassed, like I should have more control than that. Usually I end up promising myself, “That’s it, I’m not doing this again,” but I’ve made that promise so many times that even saying it makes me feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed anything specific that I would call an “issue,” exactly. It’s not something I’ve been thinking about in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I guess it depends what you mean by issues — like desire, or problems physically, or problems with a partner? I don’t really have a neat answer for that right now. Nothing has stood out to me recently where I thought, “This is a problem I need to bring up.”\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes ever happen in relation to sexual activity or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nI’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s something I’m missing. But I can’t really connect the eating episodes to sexual activity or intimacy in any clear way. It’s not like I have something happen in that area and then I go eat, or that the eating happens before or after intimacy as a pattern. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nI guess intimacy can be a complicated subject in general, and it’s a little uncomfortable to talk about, but in terms of the eating itself… no, I don’t have a specific link I can point to. If there is one, I haven’t recognized it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear decrease, like I could say, “Yes, it used to be one way and now it’s different.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head.\n\nI guess I can say it hasn’t really stood out to me as a specific problem recently. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. It’s hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any connection between your eating episodes and your feelings about sex or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as being connected in a direct way.\n\nI mean, I can see why you’re asking, because they’re both kind of personal and they both have to do with how I feel in my body, or how comfortable I feel, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed, like, if something happens around sex or intimacy, then I have an eating episode afterward. I can’t point to that kind of pattern.\n\nThere are times where I feel more distant or not very connected, and there are times where I eat in a way that feels out of control, but I don’t know that one causes the other. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. I don’t know that I’d say “usually,” no. I mean, I can see how it might look that way from the outside, or even afterward when I’m trying to make sense of it, because sometimes there probably is some feeling underneath it. But in the moment, I don’t always have that clear sense of, “I’m upset, and now I’m going to eat.”

Sometimes I’ll realize later that maybe I was tense, or bothered by something, or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but it’s not always like I’m crying or angry or obviously distressed right before it happens. A lot of times it’s more vague than that. It can feel like I just drift into it, almost, and then afterward I’m trying to figure out what set it off.

So I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed emotional upset as the usual trigger, exactly. It may be part of it sometimes, but I can’t say it’s the thing that usually starts it.

I make these promises to myself, like, “Tomorrow I’m going to be normal, I’m not going to do this again, I’m going to handle it better.” But in that moment after, mostly I just feel disgusted with myself and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during or after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Mostly after, though honestly sometimes even while it’s happening there’s this voice in my head already telling me, “You’re going to feel terrible about this,” but I still keep going. Afterward is when it really lands.\n\nI’ll look at what I ate and think about how some of it was food I meant to have for later, or something I bought for the house, or even something I thought I might share, and then I feel just awful. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. It’s hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any connection between your eating episodes and your feelings about sex or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as being connected in a direct way.\n\nI mean, I can see why you’re asking, because they’re both kind of personal and they both have to do with how I feel in my body, or how comfortable I feel, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed, like, if something happens around sex or intimacy, then I have an eating episode afterward. I can’t point to that kind of pattern.\n\nThere are times where I feel more distant or not very connected, and there are times where I eat in a way that feels out of control, but I don’t know that one causes the other. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. It’s not something I’m aware of as a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel emotionally upset right before one of these eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m usually emotionally upset right before. Sometimes I might be, but it’s not like I can consistently look back and say, “Yes, I was really upset, and then that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more unclear than that. Like I might feel kind of off, or bored, or restless, or just not very settled in myself, but I don’t always recognize it as being emotionally upset in the moment. And sometimes it starts almost without me thinking very much at all, and then afterward I’m the one trying to analyze it, like, “Was I upset? Was something bothering me? Did something happen earlier?” But I’m not always sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes there’s something emotional there, but I haven’t noticed that I’m usually upset right before. It’s more like I realize something wasn’t quite right after the fact, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can name one specific emotional state, no. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it’s always when I’m sad,” or “it’s always when I’m anxious,” or something like that. But it doesn’t seem that clear to me.\n\nSometimes before it happens I might feel kind of unsettled, or like I’m avoiding something, or just not comfortable in my own head, but other times I don’t notice anything in particular until afterward. Then I start replaying the day and wondering, “Was I upset? Was I lonely? Was I frustrated?” But in the moment, it’s not always obvious.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific feeling that reliably comes before it. It feels more mixed up than that, or maybe I’m just not good at recognizing it before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that you experience most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t know if I’d call it a lack of interest exactly, or if it’s more that it just isn’t something I think about very much, or it depends on what else is going on with me.\n\nMost of the time… I guess I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I also haven’t really tracked it that way, like “how often am I interested versus not interested.” It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured in my head. Sometimes I think if someone asked me out of nowhere, I’d say I’m not that interested, but then other times it might not feel so absolute.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer. I think I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, and I’m not sure whether it’s most of the time or just certain times. It’s kind of hard for me to separate interest in sex from how I’m feeling generally, or how comfortable I feel, or just whether I even have the mental space for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “usually,” no. I mean, I can see how it might look that way from the outside, or even afterward when I’m trying to make sense of it, because sometimes there probably is some feeling underneath it. But in the moment, I don’t always have that clear sense of, “I’m upset, and now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes I’ll realize later that maybe I was tense, or bothered by something, or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but it’s not always like I’m crying or angry or obviously distressed right before it happens. A lot of times it’s more vague than that. It can feel like I just drift into it, almost, and then afterward I’m trying to figure out what set it off.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed emotional upset as the usual trigger, exactly. It may be part of it sometimes, but I can’t say it’s the thing that usually starts it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I think if someone asked me out of nowhere, I’d say I’m not that interested, but then other times it might not feel so absolute.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer. I think I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, and I’m not sure whether it’s most of the time or just certain times. It’s kind of hard for me to separate interest in sex from how I’m feeling generally, or how comfortable I feel, or just whether I even have the mental space for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “usually,” no. I mean, I can see how it might look that way from the outside, or even afterward when I’m trying to make sense of it, because sometimes there probably is some feeling underneath it. But in the moment, I don’t always have that clear sense of, “I’m upset, and now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes I’ll realize later that maybe I was tense, or bothered by something, or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but it’s not always like I’m crying or angry or obviously distressed right before it happens. A lot of times it’s more vague than that. It can feel like I just drift into it, almost, and then afterward I’m trying to figure out what set it off.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed emotional upset as the usual trigger, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really connected those two things in my mind before — like whether the eating episodes happen more when I’m not interested in sex. I’m trying to think about it now, and it’s hard because I don’t really track either of those things that clearly.

But I don’t know if one is related to the other, or if they’re both just kind of happening around the same general state of mind.

So no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that the episodes are more likely when I’m not interested in sex. It’s possible, but I wouldn’t want to say yes when I really haven’t paid attention to it that way.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m really scared by what happens when I start eating sometimes. It’s not just, you know, “I had a snack” or “I ate more than I meant to.” It’s like once I start, especially if I’m by myself in the kitchen, something just shifts. I feel like I’m watching myself do it, almost like I’ve stepped aside, and I can’t get myself to stop.\n\nAnd the whole time, I’m thinking, “Janet, put it away. Close the cabinet. Walk out of the kitchen.” I’m saying that in my head over and over, and I still keep going. That’s the part that really scares me, because it feels like I’m not in charge of myself in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like, and that’s the part that scares me the most. It’s not like I’m just choosing to have a little more and then I’m done. Once I start, especially if I’m alone in the kitchen, it feels like something kind of takes over, or like I’ve stepped aside from myself.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the cabinet open or at the counter, and in my head I’m saying, Janet, put it away. Stop. Close it. Walk out. And I really mean it — I’m not pretending I want to stop. I do want to stop. But my hands keep reaching, I keep eating, and it feels like I can’t make myself listen to me. It’s almost like there’s this gap between what I’m telling myself to do and what I’m actually doing, and I don’t know how to close it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… awful. Really awful. It’s not even just that my stomach feels bad or that I ate too much — it’s more this heavy feeling about myself, like, “What is wrong with me?” I’ll look at what’s gone, and sometimes it’s food I bought for the house or something I meant to share, and then I feel terrible, like I’ve wasted it or taken it from someone else even if no one says that.\n\nAnd then I get this shame, just sitting there with me. I’ll think, “Janet, you are too old to be acting like this. You’re a grown woman. I make these promises to myself, like, “Tomorrow I’m going to be normal, I’m not going to do this again, I’m going to handle it better.” But in that moment after, mostly I just feel disgusted with myself and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during or after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Mostly after, though honestly sometimes even while it’s happening there’s this voice in my head already telling me, “You’re going to feel terrible about this,” but I still keep going. Afterward is when it really lands.\n\nI’ll look at what I ate and think about how some of it was food I meant to have for later, or something I bought for the house, or even something I thought I might share, and then I feel just awful. I’ll sit there thinking, “Janet, you’re 48 years old. You are too old to be acting this way. Why can’t you just stop?” It makes me feel childish and embarrassed, like I should have more control than that. Usually I end up promising myself, “That’s it, I’m not doing this again,” but I’ve made that promise so many times that even saying it makes me feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed anything specific that I would call an “issue,” exactly. It’s not something I’ve been thinking about in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I guess it depends what you mean by issues — like desire, or problems physically, or problems with a partner? I don’t really have a neat answer for that right now. Nothing has stood out to me recently where I thought, “This is a problem I need to bring up.”\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes ever happen in relation to sexual activity or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nI’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s something I’m missing. But I can’t really connect the eating episodes to sexual activity or intimacy in any clear way. It’s not like I have something happen in that area and then I go eat, or that the eating happens before or after intimacy as a pattern. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nI guess intimacy can be a complicated subject in general, and it’s a little uncomfortable to talk about, but in terms of the eating itself… no, I don’t have a specific link I can point to. If there is one, I haven’t recognized it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear decrease, like I could say, “Yes, it used to be one way and now it’s different.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head.\n\nI guess I can say it hasn’t really stood out to me as a specific problem recently. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. Was I frustrated?” But in the moment, it’s not always obvious.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific feeling that reliably comes before it. It’s not even just that my stomach feels bad or that I ate too much — it’s more this heavy feeling about myself, like, “What is wrong with me?” I’ll look at what’s gone, and sometimes it’s food I bought for the house or something I meant to share, and then I feel terrible, like I’ve wasted it or taken it from someone else even if no one says that.\n\nAnd then I get this shame, just sitting there with me. I’ll think, “Janet, you are too old to be acting like this. You’re a grown woman. Why can’t you just stop?” It’s embarrassing even saying that out loud, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nUsually I just sit there afterward and feel ashamed, and I tell myself this is the last time. I make these promises to myself, like, “Tomorrow I’m going to be normal, I’m not going to do this again, I’m going to handle it better.” But in that moment after, mostly I just feel disgusted with myself and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during or after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Mostly after, though honestly sometimes even while it’s happening there’s this voice in my head already telling me, “You’re going to feel terrible about this,” but I still keep going. Afterward is when it really lands.\n\nI’ll look at what I ate and think about how some of it was food I meant to have for later, or something I bought for the house, or even something I thought I might share, and then I feel just awful. Like I’ve wasted it, or like I’ve done something selfish, even if no one else is there saying that to me. I say it to myself.\n\nAnd then there’s this shame that comes over me. I’ll sit there thinking, “Janet, you’re 48 years old. You are too old to be acting this way. Why can’t you just stop?” It makes me feel childish and embarrassed, like I should have more control than that. Usually I end up promising myself, “That’s it, I’m not doing this again,” but I’ve made that promise so many times that even saying it makes me feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed anything specific that I would call an “issue,” exactly. It’s not something I’ve been thinking about in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I guess it depends what you mean by issues — like desire, or problems physically, or problems with a partner? I don’t really have a neat answer for that right now. Nothing has stood out to me recently where I thought, “This is a problem I need to bring up.”\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes ever happen in relation to sexual activity or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nI’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s something I’m missing. But I can’t really connect the eating episodes to sexual activity or intimacy in any clear way. It’s not like I have something happen in that area and then I go eat, or that the eating happens before or after intimacy as a pattern. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nI guess intimacy can be a complicated subject in general, and it’s a little uncomfortable to talk about, but in terms of the eating itself… no, I don’t have a specific link I can point to. If there is one, I haven’t recognized it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear decrease, like I could say, “Yes, it used to be one way and now it’s different.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head.\n\nI guess I can say it hasn’t really stood out to me as a specific problem recently. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. It’s hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any connection between your eating episodes and your feelings about sex or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as being connected in a direct way.\n\nI mean, I can see why you’re asking, because they’re both kind of personal and they both have to do with how I feel in my body, or how comfortable I feel, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed, like, if something happens around sex or intimacy, then I have an eating episode afterward. I can’t point to that kind of pattern.\n\nThere are times where I feel more distant or not very connected, and there are times where I eat in a way that feels out of control, but I don’t know that one causes the other. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. It’s not something I’m aware of as a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel emotionally upset right before one of these eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m usually emotionally upset right before. Sometimes I might be, but it’s not like I can consistently look back and say, “Yes, I was really upset, and then that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more unclear than that. Like I might feel kind of off, or bored, or restless, or just not very settled in myself, but I don’t always recognize it as being emotionally upset in the moment. And sometimes it starts almost without me thinking very much at all, and then afterward I’m the one trying to analyze it, like, “Was I upset? Was something bothering me? Did something happen earlier?” But I’m not always sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes there’s something emotional there, but I haven’t noticed that I’m usually upset right before. It’s more like I realize something wasn’t quite right after the fact, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can name one specific emotional state, no. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it’s always when I’m sad,” or “it’s always when I’m anxious,” or something like that. But it doesn’t seem that clear to me.\n\nSometimes before it happens I might feel kind of unsettled, or like I’m avoiding something, or just not comfortable in my own head, but other times I don’t notice anything in particular until afterward. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m really scared by what happens when I start eating sometimes. It’s not just, you know, “I had a snack” or “I ate more than I meant to.” It’s like once I start, especially if I’m by myself in the kitchen, something just shifts. I feel like I’m watching myself do it, almost like I’ve stepped aside, and I can’t get myself to stop.\n\nAnd the whole time, I’m thinking, “Janet, put it away. Close the cabinet. Walk out of the kitchen.” I’m saying that in my head over and over, and I still keep going. That’s the part that really scares me, because it feels like I’m not in charge of myself in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like, and that’s the part that scares me the most. It’s not like I’m just choosing to have a little more and then I’m done. Once I start, especially if I’m alone in the kitchen, it feels like something kind of takes over, or like I’ve stepped aside from myself.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the cabinet open or at the counter, and in my head I’m saying, Janet, put it away. Stop. Close it. Walk out. And I really mean it — I’m not pretending I want to stop. I do want to stop. But my hands keep reaching, I keep eating, and it feels like I can’t make myself listen to me. It’s almost like there’s this gap between what I’m telling myself to do and what I’m actually doing, and I don’t know how to close it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… awful. Really awful. It’s not even just that my stomach feels bad or that I ate too much — it’s more this heavy feeling about myself, like, “What is wrong with me?” I’ll look at what’s gone, and sometimes it’s food I bought for the house or something I meant to share, and then I feel terrible, like I’ve wasted it or taken it from someone else even if no one says that.\n\nAnd then I get this shame, just sitting there with me. I’ll think, “Janet, you are too old to be acting like this. You’re a grown woman. Why can’t you just stop?” It’s embarrassing even saying that out loud, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nUsually I just sit there afterward and feel ashamed, and I tell myself this is the last time. I make these promises to myself, like, “Tomorrow I’m going to be normal, I’m not going to do this again, I’m going to handle it better.” But in that moment after, mostly I just feel disgusted with myself and sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty during or after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… very guilty. Mostly after, though honestly sometimes even while it’s happening there’s this voice in my head already telling me, “You’re going to feel terrible about this,” but I still keep going. Afterward is when it really lands.\n\nI’ll look at what I ate and think about how some of it was food I meant to have for later, or something I bought for the house, or even something I thought I might share, and then I feel just awful. Like I’ve wasted it, or like I’ve done something selfish, even if no one else is there saying that to me. I say it to myself.\n\nAnd then there’s this shame that comes over me. I’ll sit there thinking, “Janet, you’re 48 years old. You are too old to be acting this way. Why can’t you just stop?” It makes me feel childish and embarrassed, like I should have more control than that. Usually I end up promising myself, “That’s it, I’m not doing this again,” but I’ve made that promise so many times that even saying it makes me feel worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I haven’t noticed anything specific that I would call an “issue,” exactly. It’s not something I’ve been thinking about in a clear way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, I guess it depends what you mean by issues — like desire, or problems physically, or problems with a partner? I don’t really have a neat answer for that right now. Nothing has stood out to me recently where I thought, “This is a problem I need to bring up.”\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes ever happen in relation to sexual activity or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed, anyway.\n\nI’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there’s something I’m missing. But I can’t really connect the eating episodes to sexual activity or intimacy in any clear way. It’s not like I have something happen in that area and then I go eat, or that the eating happens before or after intimacy as a pattern. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nI guess intimacy can be a complicated subject in general, and it’s a little uncomfortable to talk about, but in terms of the eating itself… no, I don’t have a specific link I can point to. If there is one, I haven’t recognized it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear decrease, like I could say, “Yes, it used to be one way and now it’s different.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head.\n\nI guess I can say it hasn’t really stood out to me as a specific problem recently. I mean, there are times when I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I don’t know if that’s a change or just… normal ups and downs, or me not paying much attention to it. I don’t want to give you a definite answer if I’m not actually sure.\n\nSo I’d say no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there has been a decrease, I haven’t really identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt less interested in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly, no. I mean, I’m 48, so part of me wonders if things are just different than they were when I was younger, but I haven’t really sat down and compared it in that way.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve been thinking, “Oh, I’m much less interested in sex now,” or that it’s been bothering me as a specific change. Sometimes I’m just tired or distracted or not really in that mindset, but I don’t know if that means less interest than before, exactly. I guess I haven’t noticed a definite drop that I could point to.\n\nSo… I’d say I’m not sure, but not in a way that feels like a clear problem to me right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have little to no interest in sexual activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “little to no interest,” no. That feels stronger than what I’m actually experiencing, if that makes sense.\n\nThere are definitely times when I’m just not thinking about sex much, or it’s not at the front of my mind, but I don’t know that I’d describe myself as having no interest. It’s more like it depends on what else is going on, how I’m feeling, whether I’m tired, that kind of thing. And I’m not even sure I have a great baseline for what “often” means here, because I haven’t really been measuring it or comparing myself to anyone.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that I often have little to no interest. I wouldn’t say it’s been standing out to me as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that happens all the time\n\nPatient: All the time? No, I don’t think I’d say that. I mean, I’m not even sure I would call it a “lack of interest,” exactly, because that makes it sound like it’s just absent, and I don’t think that’s right.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes it’s just not something I’m focused on, or it feels kind of low on the list, depending on the day and what else is going on. But not all the time, no. I haven’t noticed it as this constant thing where I’m never interested or I’m always avoiding it. If it happens, it feels more occasional or situational, not like a steady, every-day problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as stress, anxiety, or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve tried to figure that out because it seems like there should be some clear pattern, like, “Oh, I eat like that when I’m stressed,” or “I do it when I’m sad,” but when I think back on it, it’s not always that neat.\n\nSometimes I might be stressed or kind of keyed up, and then I realize I’m eating more than I meant to, but other times I don’t feel like I can point to a feeling beforehand. It’s more like I only notice afterward and then I’m trying to reconstruct what was going on in my head. And I don’t always come up with anything specific. I might just feel restless, or tired, or like I want something, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or sadness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed one specific emotion that reliably triggers it. There may be emotions involved sometimes, but I’m not confident enough to say, “Yes, it’s definitely stress or anxiety or sadness.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes happen when you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know that sounds vague, but I honestly don’t always have a clear read on it in the moment.\n\nThere are times where, looking back, I think, “Maybe I was feeling something and didn’t realize it,” like maybe I was overwhelmed or irritated or just kind of unsettled. But I don’t always sit down to eat thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat.” It’s usually less obvious than that. Sometimes I only notice afterward that I might have been feeling off, and then I start trying to piece it together.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a consistent pattern where it clearly happens whenever I’m emotional. It’s hard for me to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any connection between your eating episodes and your feelings about sex or intimacy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as being connected in a direct way.\n\nI mean, I can see why you’re asking, because they’re both kind of personal and they both have to do with how I feel in my body, or how comfortable I feel, I guess. But I don’t know that I’ve noticed, like, if something happens around sex or intimacy, then I have an eating episode afterward. I can’t point to that kind of pattern.\n\nThere are times where I feel more distant or not very connected, and there are times where I eat in a way that feels out of control, but I don’t know that one causes the other. It’s hard because after the eating happens, I’ll sometimes try to figure out what set it off, and I can come up with a lot of possibilities, but I don’t want to say there’s a connection if I’m just guessing.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be… maybe there could be some connection underneath it, but I haven’t noticed one clearly. It’s not something I’m aware of as a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel emotionally upset right before one of these eating episodes happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m usually emotionally upset right before. Sometimes I might be, but it’s not like I can consistently look back and say, “Yes, I was really upset, and then that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more unclear than that. Like I might feel kind of off, or bored, or restless, or just not very settled in myself, but I don’t always recognize it as being emotionally upset in the moment. And sometimes it starts almost without me thinking very much at all, and then afterward I’m the one trying to analyze it, like, “Was I upset? Was something bothering me? Did something happen earlier?” But I’m not always sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes there’s something emotional there, but I haven’t noticed that I’m usually upset right before. It’s more like I realize something wasn’t quite right after the fact, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can name one specific emotional state, no. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it’s always when I’m sad,” or “it’s always when I’m anxious,” or something like that. But it doesn’t seem that clear to me.\n\nSometimes before it happens I might feel kind of unsettled, or like I’m avoiding something, or just not comfortable in my own head, but other times I don’t notice anything in particular until afterward. Then I start replaying the day and wondering, “Was I upset? Was I lonely? Was I frustrated?” But in the moment, it’s not always obvious.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific feeling that reliably comes before it. It feels more mixed up than that, or maybe I’m just not good at recognizing it before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that you experience most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t know if I’d call it a lack of interest exactly, or if it’s more that it just isn’t something I think about very much, or it depends on what else is going on with me.\n\nMost of the time… I guess I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I also haven’t really tracked it that way, like “how often am I interested versus not interested.” It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured in my head. Sometimes I think if someone asked me out of nowhere, I’d say I’m not that interested, but then other times it might not feel so absolute.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer. I think I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, and I’m not sure whether it’s most of the time or just certain times. It’s kind of hard for me to separate interest in sex from how I’m feeling generally, or how comfortable I feel, or just whether I even have the mental space for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “usually,” no. I mean, I can see how it might look that way from the outside, or even afterward when I’m trying to make sense of it, because sometimes there probably is some feeling underneath it. But in the moment, I don’t always have that clear sense of, “I’m upset, and now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes I’ll realize later that maybe I was tense, or bothered by something, or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but it’s not always like I’m crying or angry or obviously distressed right before it happens. A lot of times it’s more vague than that. It can feel like I just drift into it, almost, and then afterward I’m trying to figure out what set it off.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed emotional upset as the usual trigger, exactly. It may be part of it sometimes, but I can’t say it’s the thing that usually starts it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are more likely to happen when you are not interested in sex?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really connected those two things in my mind before — like whether the eating episodes happen more when I’m not interested in sex. I’m trying to think about it now, and it’s hard because I don’t really track either of those things that clearly.\n\nI guess sometimes when I’m feeling disconnected from myself, or just not very present in my body, both things could be true at the same time — I might not feel interested in sex, and I might also be more likely to eat in that out-of-control way. But I don’t know if one is related to the other, or if they’re both just kind of happening around the same general state of mind.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that the episodes are more likely when I’m not interested in sex. It’s possible, but I wouldn’t want to say yes when I really haven’t paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that has been consistent over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s been consistent, exactly. I don’t really have a clear timeline in my head where I could say, “Yes, this has always been the same,” or “No, it definitely changed at this point.” It’s more like… I know there have been periods where sex just wasn’t something I felt very drawn to or thought about much, but I don’t know if that means it’s consistent over time or if it just comes and goes depending on what else is happening.\n\nI guess part of the problem is I don’t really monitor it unless someone asks me. So looking back, it all feels kind of blurry. There are times when I think, “Maybe I’ve always been kind of this way,” but then I also wonder if I’m just remembering it that way because that’s how I feel now. I don’t want to give you an answer that sounds more definite than it is.\n\nSo… I’d say I haven’t noticed a very clear pattern. And sometimes it starts almost without me thinking very much at all, and then afterward I’m the one trying to analyze it, like, “Was I upset? Was something bothering me? Did something happen earlier?” But I’m not always sure.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes there’s something emotional there, but I haven’t noticed that I’m usually upset right before. It’s more like I realize something wasn’t quite right after the fact, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can name one specific emotional state, no. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would almost be easier if I could say, “Oh, it’s always when I’m sad,” or “it’s always when I’m anxious,” or something like that. But it doesn’t seem that clear to me.\n\nSometimes before it happens I might feel kind of unsettled, or like I’m avoiding something, or just not comfortable in my own head, but other times I don’t notice anything in particular until afterward. Then I start replaying the day and wondering, “Was I upset? Was I lonely? Was I frustrated?” But in the moment, it’s not always obvious.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a specific feeling that reliably comes before it. It feels more mixed up than that, or maybe I’m just not good at recognizing it before it happens.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in sex something that you experience most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t know if I’d call it a lack of interest exactly, or if it’s more that it just isn’t something I think about very much, or it depends on what else is going on with me.\n\nMost of the time… I guess I don’t feel especially focused on sex, but I also haven’t really tracked it that way, like “how often am I interested versus not interested.” It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured in my head. Sometimes I think if someone asked me out of nowhere, I’d say I’m not that interested, but then other times it might not feel so absolute.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer. I think I’d say I haven’t noticed a clear pattern, and I’m not sure whether it’s most of the time or just certain times. It’s kind of hard for me to separate interest in sex from how I’m feeling generally, or how comfortable I feel, or just whether I even have the mental space for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “usually,” no. I mean, I can see how it might look that way from the outside, or even afterward when I’m trying to make sense of it, because sometimes there probably is some feeling underneath it. But in the moment, I don’t always have that clear sense of, “I’m upset, and now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes I’ll realize later that maybe I was tense, or bothered by something, or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but it’s not always like I’m crying or angry or obviously distressed right before it happens. It can feel like I just drift into it, almost, and then afterward I’m trying to figure out what set it off.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed emotional upset as the usual trigger, exactly. It may be part of it sometimes, but I can’t say it’s the thing that usually starts it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are more likely to happen when you are not interested in sex?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I haven’t really connected those two things in my mind before — like whether the eating episodes happen more when I’m not interested in sex. I’m trying to think about it now, and it’s hard because I don’t really track either of those things that clearly.\n\nI guess sometimes when I’m feeling disconnected from myself, or just not very present in my body, both things could be true at the same time — I might not feel interested in sex, and I might also be more likely to eat in that out-of-control way. But I don’t know if one is related to the other, or if they’re both just kind of happening around the same general state of mind.\n\nSo no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that the episodes are more likely when I’m not interested in sex.
